be a global trivialization of the contact structure
\xi
on
Y
. Via
\tau
, we can regard
\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}v(s,\cdot))
as a function in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2})
for all
s
such that
\left\{s\right\}\times\mathbb{T}
does not contain a puncture. For all
s\gg 0
sufficiently large, this function is nowhere vanishing, i.e. takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
, and we have
\operatorname{wind}_{\infty}^{\tau}(v,\infty)=\operatorname{wind}(\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}v(s,\cdot)))
. Let us fix such a value
s_{0}\gg 0
. After a suitable reparametrization of the planes
u_{k}
, we can assume that
u_{k}
converges to
v
in
C^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma)
, up to translation along the
\mathbb{R}
-factor of the symplectization
\mathbb{R}\times Y
. In particular, we have convergence
\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}u_{k}(s_{0},\cdot))\rightarrow\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}v(s_{0},\cdot))
in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
. For all sufficiently large
k\gg 0
, the function
\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}u_{k}(s_{0},\cdot))
must therefore be nowhere vanishing and
\operatorname{wind}(\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}u_{k}(s_{0},\cdot)))=\operatorname{wind}(\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}v(s_{0},\cdot)))
. Since the section
\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}u_{k})
is in the kernel of some Cauchy-Riemann type operator, its zeros are isolated and have positive multiplicities. Moreover,
\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}u_{k}(s,\cdot))
is nowhere vanishing for all
s\gg 0
and since the planes
u_{k}
are fast, we have
\operatorname{wind}(\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}u_{k}(s,\cdot)))\leq 1
. For
s>s_{0}
, the difference
\operatorname{wind}(\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}u_{k}(s,\cdot)))-\operatorname{wind}(\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}u_{k}(s_{0},\cdot)))
agrees with the count of zeros of
\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}u_{k})
in the region
[s_{0},s]\times\mathbb{T}
, which is non-negative. We conclude that

\operatorname{wind}_{\infty}^{\tau}(v,\infty)=\operatorname{wind}(\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}v(s_{0},\cdot)))=\operatorname{wind}(\pi_{\xi}(\partial_{s}u_{k}(s_{0},\cdot)))\leq 1.

We recall that

0\leq\operatorname{wind}_{\pi}(v)=\operatorname{wind}_{\infty}^{\tau}(v,\infty)-\sum\limits_{z\in\Gamma}\operatorname{wind}_{\infty}^{\tau}(v,z)-\chi(\mathbb{C}\setminus\Gamma).

Dynamical convexity up to action
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
implies that
\operatorname{wind}_{\infty}^{\tau}(v,z)\geq 2
for all
z\in\Gamma
. Therefore, the right hand side of the above equality is bounded from above by
1-2\#\Gamma-(1-\#\Gamma)=-\#\Gamma
. We conclude that
v
does not have any negative punctures and that
\operatorname{wind}_{\infty}(v)=1
. Thus
v\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)
.

It remains to consider the case that the top layer
v
is contained in
\hat{X}
. Clearly,
v
must be a pseudoholomorphic plane positively asymptotic to
\gamma
. We need to verify that
v
is fast. This is the case if
v
has trivial asymptotic behaviour by definition. So assume that
v
has non-trival asymptotic behaviour. Arguing exactly as before, one can show that
\operatorname{wind}_{\infty}(v)\leq 1
. This shows that
v
is fast and concludes the proof of the second assertion.
∎

In addition, it will be useful to introduce moduli spaces of unparametrized fast pseudoholomorphic planes with one marked point

\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J}).

Elements of
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)
are represented by pairs
(u,z)
consisting of a parametrized fast pseudoholomorphic plane
u:(\mathbb{C},i)\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}\times Y,J)
positively asymptotic to
\gamma
and a point
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Two such pairs
(u_{1},z_{1})
and
(u_{2},z_{2})
are considered to be equivalent if there exists a biholomorphism
\varphi
of
\mathbb{C}
such that
u_{2}=u_{1}\circ\varphi
and
\varphi(z_{2})=z_{1}
. Elements of
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,J)
have an analogous description. There is a natural forgetful map
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)
forgetting the marked point. As observed above, all pseudoholomorphic planes asymptotic to
\gamma
are somewhere injective. Thus the fibres of this forgetful map are simply given by
\mathbb{C}
. The same is true for the forgetful map on
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
. We have well-defined evaluation maps

\displaystyle\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}:\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times Y\qquad(u,z)

\displaystyle\mapsto u(z)

\displaystyle\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}:\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})\rightarrow\hat{X}\qquad(u,z)

\displaystyle\mapsto u(z).

Note that
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}
is equivariant with respect to the natural
\mathbb{R}
-actions on
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)
and
\mathbb{R}\times Y
by translation.

Proposition 3.4

(Properness
[
HSS22
, Lemma 4.9]
)
.

The following statements are true.

(i)

Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}\times(Y\setminus\gamma)
be a compact subset. Then the preimage of
K
under the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}
is compact.

(ii)

The evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
is proper.

Proof.

Both assertions are a straightforward application of Proposition
3.3
. The first assertion also follows from
[
HSS22
, Lemma 4.9]
. We present a proof of the second assertion.

Let
K\subset\hat{X}
be a compact subset. Let
(u_{k},z_{k})\in\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}^{-1}(K)
be a sequence of fast planes with marked point. Let us temporarily ignore the marked points
z_{k}
and apply Proposition
3.3
to the underlying sequence of fast planes
u_{k}
. This allows us to pass to a subsequence which converges to a fast plane
u
positively asymptotic to
\gamma
, either in the symplectization
\mathbb{R}\times Y
or in the completion
\hat{X}
. Since each
u_{k}
passes through the compact set
K
, the same is true for the limit
u
, which therefore is contained in
\hat{X}
. Next, we apply the SFT compactness theorem to the sequence
(u_{k},z_{k})
with the marked points. After passing to a further subsequence, this sequence converges to a pseudoholomorphic building with a marked point. Since we already know that the sequence
u_{k}
converges to a single layer building consisting of a fast plane
u
positively asymptotic to
\gamma
in
\hat{X}
, there are only two possibilities for the limiting building of
(u_{k},z_{k})
: a single layer building consisting of a fast plane with marked point
(u,z)
in
\hat{X}
; or a two layer building consisting of a fast plane
u
in
\hat{X}
and the trivial cylinder over
\gamma
with marked point
(u_{\gamma},z)
in
\mathbb{R}\times Y
. We can immediately rule out the second case because all
u_{k}(z_{k})
are contained in the compact set
K
. In the first case, we must clearly have
u(z)\in K
. Therefore,
(u,z)\in\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}^{-1}(K)
is the desired limit of a subsequence of
(u_{k},z_{k})
. This shows compactness of
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}^{-1}(K)
and therefore properness of
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
.
∎

Let
A_{\gamma}
denote the asymptotic operator of the orbit
\gamma
. Since
Y
is dynamically convex up to action
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
, we have
\operatorname{CZ}_{\tau}(\gamma)\geq 3
where
\tau
is a global trivialization of the contact structure
\xi
. Equivalently, we have
\alpha_{\tau}^{<0}(A_{\gamma})\geq 1
and
\alpha_{\tau}^{\geq 0}(A_{\gamma})\geq 2
. This means that we can choose a non-negative constant
c\geq 0
such that
-c\notin\sigma(A_{\gamma})
and
\alpha_{\tau}^{<-c}(A_{\gamma})=1
and
\alpha_{\tau}^{\geq-c}(A_{\gamma})=2
. A pseudoholomorphic plane in
\mathbb{R}\times Y
or
\hat{X}
positively asymptotic to
\gamma
is fast if and only if its transversal asymptotic convergence rate is strictly bigger than
c
. Thus the Fredholm theory for pseudoholomorphic curves with asymptotic constraints which we summarized in Section
2
applies to the moduli spaces of fast pseudoholomorphic planes. Note that since
\gamma
is a simple periodic orbit, there is a unique choice of asymptotic marker for every plane asymptotic to
\gamma
and we can therefore omit asymptotic markers from our discussion.

The constrained Fredholm index of a fast plane
u
is given by

\operatorname{ind}^{c}(u)=-\chi(\mathbb{C})+\operatorname{CZ}_{\tau}^{-c}(\gamma)=2.

(3.1)

Recall that there is a normal deformation operator

D_{u}^{N}:W^{1,p,c}(N_{u})\rightarrow L^{p,c}(\Lambda^{0,1}T^{*}\mathbb{C}\otimes N_{u})

of Fredholm index

\operatorname{ind}^{c}(D_{u}^{N})=\operatorname{ind}^{c}(u)-2Z(du)

(3.2)

with the property that
u
is regular if and only if
D_{u}^{N}
is surjective. Note that the index
\operatorname{ind}^{c}(D_{u}^{N})
is always even. Therefore, the orientation line
\mathfrak{o}_{D_{u}^{N}}
admits a canonical complex orientation by Remark
1.2
. This induces a canonical orientation of our moduli spaces of fast planes, as explained at the end of Section
2.5
. In the following, we equip the moduli spaces with this orientation.

Proposition 3.5

(Automatic regularity)
.

The following statements are true.

(i)

Every fast plane in
\mathbb{R}\times Y
is automatically regular. In particular, the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)
and
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)
are smooth manifolds of dimensions
2
and
4
, respectively.

(ii)

Every fast plane
u
in
\hat{X}
satisfying
Z(du)\in\left\{0,1\right\}
is automatically regular. In particular, open neighbourhoods of the subsets of
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
consisting of fast planes satisfying
Z(du)\in\left\{0,1\right\}
are smooth manifolds of dimensions
2
and
4
, respectively.

(iii)

The evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}
is an orientation preserving local diffeomorphisms.

(iv)

The restriction of the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
to the open subset consisting of all immersed fast planes with marked point is an orientation preserving local diffeomorphism.

(v)

Let
(u,z)\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
and assume that
Z(du)=1
. Then

\operatorname{im}d\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}(u,z)=(T_{u})_{z}\subset T_{u(z)}\hat{X}.

Proof.

Let
u
be a fast plane in
W
where
W\in\{\mathbb{R}\times Y,\hat{X}\}
. By Proposition
1.3
, the operator
D_{u}^{N}
is surjective if and only if
\operatorname{ind}^{c}(D_{u}^{N})\geq 0
. It is immediate from equations (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) that this is the case if and only if
Z(du)\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, i.e. if and only if
u
is immersed or has exactly one non-immersed point of order
1
. This shows statement (
ii
) and also statement (
i
) because fast planes in the symplectization are automatically immersed.

Next, consider an immersed fast plane with marked point
(u,z)\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(W,\gamma)
. By equations (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), we have
\operatorname{ind}^{c}(D_{u}^{N})=2
. We may split the tangent space

T_{(u,z)}\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\operatorname{fast}}(W,\gamma)=T_{z}\mathbb{C}\oplus\operatorname{ker}(D_{u}^{N})

(3.3)

into a component
T_{z}\mathbb{C}
tangent to the fiber of the forgetful map
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\operatorname{fast}}(W,\gamma)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}(W,\gamma)
and a component
\operatorname{ker}(D_{u}^{N})
transverse to the fiber. The linearization of the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{W}
at
(u,z)
is given by

d\operatorname{ev}_{W}(u,z):T_{z}\mathbb{C}\oplus\operatorname{ker}(D_{u}^{N})\rightarrow(T_{u})_{z}\oplus(N_{u})_{z}=T_{u(z)}W\qquad(w,X)\mapsto(du(z)w,X(z)).

(3.4)

The first component of this linear map is complex linear and bijective because
u
is immersed by assumption. The second component is bijective and orientation preserving by Proposition
1.3
. Therefore,
d\operatorname{ev}_{W}(u,z)
is invertible and orientation preserving. This implies statements (
iii
) and (
iv
).

Assume now that
(u,z)\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
satisfies
Z(du)=1
. In this case, the normal operator
D_{u}^{N}
has index
0
and is therefore bijective. The tangent part of the deformation operator
D_{u}
is the Cauchy-Riemann operator
\overline{\partial}_{T_{u}}
induced by the holomorphic structure of
T_{u}

\overline{\partial}_{T_{u}}:V_{\gamma}^{T}\oplus W^{1,p,\delta}(T_{u})\rightarrow L^{p,\delta}(\Lambda^{0,1}T^{*}\mathbb{C}\otimes T_{u}).

It is surjective and has index
6
. We can explicitly identify its kernel. Let
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
denote the unique critical point of
du
. Then
\operatorname{ker}\overline{\partial}_{T_{u}}
consists of all sections of
T_{u}
of the form

\mathbb{C}\ni z\mapsto du(z)(a_{1}(z-z_{0})+a_{0}+a_{-1}(z-z_{0})^{-1})\in(T_{u})_{z}

(3.5)

where
a_{1},a_{0},a_{-1}\in\mathbb{C}
. Note that (
3.5
) is a well-defined section of
T_{u}
because in a holomorphic chart near
z_{0}
, the derivative of
u
can be written as
du(z)=(z-z_{0})F(z)
where
F
is holomorphic and satisfies
F(z_{0})\neq 0
. For every fixed
z\in\mathbb{C}
, the map

\mathbb{C}^{3}\ni(a_{1},a_{0},a_{-1})\mapsto du(z)(a_{1}(z-z_{0})+a_{0}+a_{-1}(z-z_{0})^{-1})\in(T_{u})_{z}

(3.6)

is surjective. If
z
is not a zero of
du
, this is clear because
(T_{u})_{z}=\operatorname{im}du(z)
. If
z=z_{0}
is the unique zero of
du
, write
du(z)=(z-z_{0})F(z)
as above and recall that
(T_{u})_{z_{0}}=\mathbb{C}\cdot F(z_{0})
by definition. Surjectivity of the map (
3.6
) implies that the image of
d\operatorname{ev}_{W}(u,z)
is equal to
(T_{u})_{z}
.
∎

Proposition 3.6

(Generic regularity)
.

Suppose that the almost complex structure
\hat{J}
on
\hat{X}
is generic or a member of a generic
1
-parameter family. Then every
u\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
satisfies
Z(du)\in\left\{0,1\right\}
. Moreover, the set of
u
satisfying
Z(du)=1
is finite.

Remark 3.7
.

Note that any fast plane in
\hat{X}
which does not intersect the interior of
X
is automatically immersed by Remark
3.2
. In Proposition
3.6
it is therefore possible to fix the almost complex structure
\hat{J}
on the positive cylindrical end
X^{+}=[0,\infty)\times Y
and only make it generic in the interior of
X
.

Proof.

We assume by contradiction that
u\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
satisfies
Z(du)\geq 2
. By equations (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), the normal operator
D_{u}^{N}
has index at most
-2
. Hence the image of
D_{u}^{N}
has codimension at least
2
and the same must be true for the image of the full deformation operator
D_{u}
. Recall that
u
is somewhere injective because
\gamma
is a simple orbit. Thus it follows from the assumption that
\hat{J}
is contained in a generic
1
-parameter family that the image of
D_{u}
has codimension at most
1
. This is a contradiction and we conclude that
u
cannot exist.

It remains to argue that the set of fast planes
u
satisfying
Z(du)=1
is finite. Consider the moduli space

\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{\operatorname{crit}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})\coloneqq\left\{(u,z)\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})\mid du(z)=0\right\}.

We claim that this moduli space is compact. Indeed, let
(u_{k},z_{k})
be a sequence in
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{\operatorname{crit}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
. After passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
(u_{k},z_{k})
converges in the sense of SFT to a limiting pseudoholomorphic building with one marked point. By Proposition
3.3
, the sequence
u_{k}
without the marked point converges to a fast plane
u
either in
\hat{X}
or in
\mathbb{R}\times Y
. This results in the following possible options for the SFT limit of the sequence
(u_{k},z_{k})
with marked point: a single level building consisting of a fast plane with marked point
(u,z)
asymptotic to
\gamma
in
\hat{X}
or in
\mathbb{R}\times Y
; or a two level building with bottom level a fast plane
u
asymptotic to
\gamma
in
\hat{X}
or
\mathbb{R}\times Y
and top level the trivial cylinder with marked point
(u_{\gamma},z)
over
\gamma
in
\mathbb{R}\times Y
. Since
du_{\gamma}(z)\neq 0
, the sequence
(u_{k},z_{k})
, which satisfies
du_{k}(z_{k})=0
, clearly cannot converge to such a two level building. Similarly, since all fast planes in symplectizations are immersed, we would have
du(z)\neq 0
in the case of a single level limiting building in the symplectization
\mathbb{R}\times Y
, also ruling out this case. Hence the limiting building must be a fast plane
(u,z)
in
\hat{X}
satisfying
du(z)=0
, i.e. an element of
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{\operatorname{crit}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
. This implies that
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{\operatorname{crit}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
is compact. Our goal is to show that it is also regular and
0
-dimensional. Finiteness is then an immediate consequence.

Let
(u,z_{0})\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{\operatorname{crit}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
. We recall from
[
Wen16
, §4.7]
that one can set up a Fredholm theory for the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{\operatorname{crit}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
such that the deformation operator
D_{u}^{\operatorname{crit}}
is simply given by the restriction of the deformation operator
D_{u}
to the subspace
V_{\gamma}\oplus W^{2,p,(\delta,c)}_{\operatorname{crit}}(u^{*}T\hat{X})
where

W^{2,p,(\delta,c)}_{\operatorname{crit}}(u^{*}T\hat{X})\coloneqq\left\{\eta\in W^{2,p,(\delta,c)}(u^{*}T\hat{X})\mid\nabla^{1,0}\eta(z_{0})=0\right\}.

Note that
W^{2,p,(\delta,c)}_{\operatorname{crit}}(u^{*}T\hat{X})
has codimension
4
in
W^{2,p,(\delta,c)}(u^{*}T\hat{X})
. Since
D_{u}
has index
6
, we conclude that
D_{u}^{\operatorname{crit}}
has index
2
. Recall from the proof of Proposition
3.5
that the kernel of
D_{u}
consists of all sections of
T_{u}
of the form

\mathbb{C}\in z\mapsto du(z)(a_{1}(z-z_{0})+a_{0}+a_{-1}(z-z_{0})^{-1})\in(T_{u})_{z}

where
a_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
. In a local holomorphic chart around
z_{0}
we can write
du(z)=(z-z_{0})F(z)
where
F
is holomorphic. A simple computation shows that an element of the kernel of
D_{u}
is contained in
V_{\gamma}\oplus W^{2,p,(\delta,c)}_{\operatorname{crit}}
if and only if

a_{0}F(z_{0})+a_{-1}F^{\prime}(z_{0})=0.

Thus the dimension of
\operatorname{ker}D_{u}^{\operatorname{crit}}
is
2
if
F(z_{0})
and
F^{\prime}(z_{0})
are linearly independent over
\mathbb{C}
and
4
otherwise. Since the index of
D_{u}^{\operatorname{crit}}
is
2
, this means that
D_{u}^{\operatorname{crit}}
is surjective in the former case and has cokernel of dimension
2
in the latter case. Since
\hat{J}
is contained in a generic
1
-parameter family, the latter case cannot happen. We conclude that the moduli space of parametrized fast planes with vanishing derivative at
z_{0}
is regular and
2
-dimensional. Note that the group of biholomorphisms of
\mathbb{C}
fixing
z_{0}
is
2
-dimensional. Hence the moduli space of unparametrized fast planes with vanishing derivative at a marked point is
0
-dimensional.
∎

By Proposition
3.5
the evaluation map

\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}:\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times Y

is an orientation preserving local diffeomorphism between smooth
4
-manifolds for every symplectization admissible almost complex structure
J
. Whenever the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)
is non-empty, the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}
restricts to a covering map

\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}|_{\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\times(Y\setminus\gamma))}:\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\times(Y\setminus\gamma))\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times(Y\setminus\gamma)

of finite positive degree by Proposition
3.4
. We let
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y})>0
denote the degree of this covering map. In the case that
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)
is empty, we set
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y})\coloneqq 0
. Note that for every point
z\in\mathbb{R}\times(Y\setminus\gamma)
, the degree
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y})
agrees with both the algebraic and the geometric count of
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}^{-1}(z)
.

If
\hat{J}
is a generic admissible almost complex structure on
\hat{X}
, then

\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}:\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})\rightarrow\hat{X}

is a proper smooth map between smooth oriented
4
-manifolds by Propositions
3.4
,
3.5
and
3.6
and as such has a well-defined degree
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}})
. It is an orientation preserving local diffeomorphism on the complement of the locus of non-immersed fast planes, which consists of finitely many fibers of the forgetful map
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
. Therefore, the degree
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}})
is non-negative and zero exactly if
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
is empty. If
z\in\hat{X}
is a point not contained in the union of the images of all non-immersed planes, which is a set of codimension
2
, then the algebraic and geometric counts of
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}^{-1}(z)
both agree with
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}})
.

Proposition 3.8

(Degree)
.

The degrees
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y})
and
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}})
agree and are independent of the choice of the almost complex structure.

Proof.

Let us begin by showing that
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y})
is independent of the choice of
J
. To this end, consider two symplectization admissible almost complex structures
J_{0}
and
J_{1}
. For
k\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, let
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}^{k}
denote the resulting evaluation maps. Connect
J_{0}
to
J_{1}
via a smooth path
(J_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
. Let
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,(J_{t})_{t\in[0,1]})
denote the moduli space consisting of all tuples
(t,(u,z))
where
t\in[0,1]
and
[u,z]\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J_{t})
. We have an evaluation map

\operatorname{ev}_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}\times Y}:\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,(J_{t})_{t\in[0,1]})\rightarrow[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}\times Y\qquad(t,(u,z))\mapsto(t,u(z)).

If follows exactly as in the proofs of Propositions
3.3
,
3.4
and
3.5
that the restriction of
\operatorname{ev}_{[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}\times Y}
to the preimage of
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}\times(Y\setminus\gamma)
is a finite covering map. Over
\left\{0,1\right\}\times\mathbb{R}\times(Y\setminus\gamma)
this covering map restricts to the covering maps induced by
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}^{0}
and
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}^{1}
, which therefore have the same degree.

Next, consider a generic admissible almost complex structure
\hat{J}
on
\hat{X}
. Let
J
be the symplectization admissible almost complex structure on
\mathbb{R}\times Y
whose restriction to
X^{+}
agrees with
\hat{J}
. Fix a point
q\in Y\setminus\gamma
. We claim that, for every sufficiently large
a\gg 0
, every fast plane in
\mathbb{R}\times Y
or
\hat{X}
asymptotic to
\gamma
and passing through
(a,q)
is contained in
X^{+}
. In the case of
\mathbb{R}\times Y
, this easily follows from Propsitions
3.4
and
3.5
. Indeed, there are only finitely many fast planes
u_{i}
passing through
(0,q)
. For every
a
, the fast planes passing through
(a,q)
are precisely the translates of
u_{i}
along the
\mathbb{R}
-factor of
\mathbb{R}\times Y
by amount
a
. For
a\gg 0
, these finitely many translates are clearly contained in
X^{+}
. Next, we consider the case of
\hat{X}
. Assume by contradiction that the claim fails. Then we may pick, for every
k\geq 0
, a fast plane with marked point
(u_{k},z_{k})
which intsects
X
and satisfies
u_{k}(z_{k})=(k,q)\in X^{+}
. By Proposition
3.3
applied to the sequence
u_{k}
without the marked points, we may pass to a subsequence which converges to a fast plane
u
in either
\hat{X}
or the symplectization
\mathbb{R}\times Y
. Since each
u_{k}
intersects
X
, the same must be true for
u
, which is therefore contained in
\hat{X}
. We apply the SFT compactness theorem to the sequence
(u_{k},z_{k})
with marked points and pass to a further subsequence which converges to a pseudoholomorphic building with marked point. As in the proof of Proposition
3.4
, there are two options for the limiting building: a single layer building consisting of a fast plane with marked point
(u,z)
in
\hat{X}
; or a two layer building consisting of a fast plane
u
in
\hat{X}
and the trivial cylinder over
\gamma
with marked point
(u_{\gamma},z)
in
\mathbb{R}\times Y
. In the first case, we have
u_{k}(z_{k})\rightarrow u(z)
as
k\rightarrow\infty
, which clearly contradicts
u_{k}(z_{k})=(k,q)
. In the second case, we have
\operatorname{pr}_{Y}(u_{k}(z_{k}))\rightarrow\operatorname{pr}_{Y}(u_{\gamma}(z))
as
k\rightarrow\infty
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{Y}:\mathbb{R}\times Y\rightarrow Y
denotes the natural projection. This is also a contradiction because
\operatorname{pr}_{Y}(u_{k}(z_{k}))=q\notin\gamma
and
\operatorname{pr}_{Y}(u_{\gamma}(z))\in\gamma
. This concludes the proof of the claim in the case of
\hat{X}
.

Let us now fix
a\gg 0
sufficiently large as above. Then we have
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}^{-1}(a,q)=\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}^{-1}(a,q)
. Since the degrees of
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}
and
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
are both given by the geometric count of this set, they must agree. Finally, we observe that since we already know that
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y})
is independent of the almost complex structure, the same must be true for
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}})
.
∎

4
Detecting Hopf orbits

The main result of this section states that the degree of the evaluation map from the moduli space of fast planes with marked point discussed in Section
3
detects Hopf orbits. Before stating it, let us make the following definition. Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a domain which is uniformly starshaped with respect to the origin and let
(Y,\xi=\operatorname{ker}\lambda)
be the contact boundary of
X
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
K\subset(Y,\xi)
be a transverse knot. We call an immersed disk
D\subset X
a positively immersed symplectic disk with boundary
K
if it satisfies the following properties:

(i)

D
is symplectic, i.e. the symplectic form on
X
restricts to an area form on
D
.

(ii)

Consider a collar neighbourhood
(-\varepsilon,0]\times Y
of
Y
induced by the radial Liouville vector field. For
\varepsilon>0
sufficietly small, the intersection of
D
with this collar neighbourhood is given by
(-\varepsilon,0]\times K
.

(iii)

All self-intersection points of
D
are positive transverse double points. Positivity means that if we orient
D
via the symplectic form, then the orientations of the two branches of
D
meeting at a double point induce the natural symplectic orientation of
X
.

Remark 4.2
.

The existence of such a positively immersed symplectic disk only depends on the transverse knot type of
K
. If
X^{\prime}
is another uniformly starshaped domain with contact boundary
Y^{\prime}
, then the radial projection
Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
is a contactomorphism. If
K\subset Y
and
K^{\prime}\subset Y^{\prime}
are two transverse knots corresponding to each other via this contactomorphism, then
K
bounds a positively immersed symplectic disk in
X
if and only if
K^{\prime}
bounds a positively immersed symplectic disk in
X^{\prime}
.

If
D
is a positively immersed symplectic disk bounded by
K
, then the self-linking number of
K
and the number of double points of
D
are related by the formula

\operatorname{sl}(K)=-1+2\cdot\#(\text{double points of $D$}),

see
[
HWZ99
, proof of Corollary 1.9]
. In particular, we see that if
K
bounds one embedded symplectic disk, then all positively immersed symplectic disks bounded by
K
are embedded.

Let us now return to the setting of Section
3
. Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a uniformly starshaped domain with boundary
Y\coloneqq\partial X
, let
\gamma
be a simple periodic orbit on
Y
, and assume that the Reeb flow on
Y
is nondegenerate and dynamically convex up to action
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
. Recall that the evaluation maps

\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}:\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\gamma,J)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times Y\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}:\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})\rightarrow\hat{X}

have well-defined non-negative finite degrees which moreover agree and are independent of the choice of almost complex structure. In the following, let us abbreviate this common degree by
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})
.

Theorem 4.3
.

The degree
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})
is non-negative, and strictly positive if and only if
\gamma
bounds a positively immersed symplectic disk. Moreover, the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})=1
;

(ii)

\gamma
bounds an embedded symplectic disk;

(iii)

\gamma
is a Hopf orbit;

(iv)

\gamma
bounds a disk-like global surface of section.

Note that Theorem
4.3
fully characterizes in purely topological terms when a simple periodic orbit on the boundary of a nondegenerate dynamically convex domain is the asymptotic limit of a fast pseudoholomorphic plane. This is particularly interesting in view of
[
HSS22
, Theorem 1.4]
. This theorem states that if a periodic orbit on a nondegenerate dynamically convex domain is the limit of a fast plane, then it actually bounds a global surface of section for the Reeb flow. We obtain the following immediate corollary:

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
be a nondegenerate dynamically convex domain and let
\gamma
be a simple periodic orbit whose underlying transverse knot type admits a positively immersed symplectic disk. Then
\gamma
bounds a global surface of section.

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

We begin by showing three claims.

Claim 4.5
.

Assume that
\gamma
bounds a positively immersed symplectic disk. Then
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})>0
.

Proof.

Starting from a positively immersed symplectic disk with boundary
\gamma
, one can construct a positively immersed symplectic plane in
\hat{X}
whose intersection with the positive cylindrical end
X^{+}
is given by the half cylinder
[0,\infty)\times\gamma
. We may pick an admissible almost complex structure
\hat{J}
on
\hat{X}
which turns this positively immersed symplectic plane into an immersed pseudoholomorphic plane
u
positively asymptotic to
\gamma
. Note that
u
has trivial asymptotic behaviour at
\gamma
and is therefore a fast plane. By assertion (
ii
) in Proposition
3.5
, the fast plane
u
is regular. It therefore persists under small perturbations of the almost complex structure and remains immersed. This shows that there exists a generic admissible almost complex structure
\hat{J}
, still denoted by the same symbol, and an immersed fast plane
u\in\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
. By Proposition
3.6
the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
only contains finitely many non-immersed planes. The union of their images cannot contain the image of the immersed plane
u
. Hence we can pick a point
z\in\mathbb{C}
such that
u(z)
is not contained in any non-immersed plane. As observed above, the degree
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})
is equal to the geometric count
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}^{-1}(u(z))
. This count is strictly positive because
(u,z)
is contained in
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}^{-1}(u(z))
.
∎

Claim 4.6
.

Assume that
\gamma
bounds a positively immersed symplectic disk which is not embedded. Then
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})>1
.

Proof.

By Claim
4.5
, the degree
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})
is non-negative. We can therefore find an immersed fast plane
u
asymptotic to
\gamma
. Since
\gamma
bounds a non-embedded positively immersed symplectic disk, the plane
u
cannot be embedded. This means that there exist distinct points
z_{1}\neq z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
u(z_{1})=u(z_{2})\eqqcolon p
. We observe that
(u,z_{1})
and
(u,z_{2})
represent distinct elements of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
. Indeed, since
u
is somewhere injective, any biholomorphism
\varphi
of
\mathbb{C}
satisfying
u\circ\varphi=u
is necessarily equal to the identity and can certainly not carry
z_{2}
to
z_{1}
. This shows that the geometric count of
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}^{-1}(p)
is strictly bigger than
1
. By assertion (
iv
) in Proposition
3.5
, the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
is a local diffeomorphism near the points
(u,z_{1})
and
(u,z_{2})
in
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
. This implies that the geometric count of
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}^{-1}(q)
is strictly bigger than
1
for all
q
sufficiently close to
p
. Since a generic such
q
is not contained in the image of any non-immersed fast plane in
\mathcal{M}^{\operatorname{fast}}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,\hat{J})
, we obtain that the degree
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})
is strictly bigger than
1
.
∎

Claim 4.7
.

Assume that
\gamma
bounds an embedded symplectic disk. Then
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
is a diffeomorphism and
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}
is a diffeomorphism onto
\mathbb{R}\times(Y\setminus\gamma)
. In particular,
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})=1
.

Proof.

The proof of this claim is probably well-known to experts. For convenience of the reader, we still carry it out. Since
\gamma
bounds an embedded symplectic disk, any pseudoholomorphic plane asymptotic to
\gamma
must be embedded. Our goal is to show that any two fast planes
u
and
v
asymptotic to
\gamma
with distinct images must in fact have disjoint images. Let us fix two such fast planes
u
and
v
. Since
u
and
v
have distinct images, at least one of them, say
u
, has non-trivial asymptotic behaviour. Let
e_{u}
and
\nu_{u}
be the asymptotic eigenfunction and eigenvalue of
u
. Moreover, let
e
and
\nu
be the relative asymptotic eigenfunction and eigenvalue of
u
and
v
. Note that we have
\nu\leq\nu_{u}<0
. Let
\tau
be a symplectic trivialization of
\gamma^{*}\xi
which extends to a global trivialization of the contact structure
\xi
on
Y
. Since
u
is a fast plane, we have
\operatorname{wind}^{\tau}(e_{u})\leq 1
by definition. Since the winding number is non-decreasing on the spectrum
\sigma(A_{\gamma})
and
\nu\leq\nu_{u}
, we obtain that
\operatorname{wind}^{\tau}(e)\leq 1
. Recall that
\operatorname{sl}(\gamma)=-1
because
\gamma
bounds an embedded symplectic disk. The self-linking number of
\gamma
can be computed as the algebraic count of intersections between
u
and a pushoff via a generic section of the normal bundle
N_{u}
which, near infinity, is non-vanishing and constant with respect to the trivialization
\tau
. This implies that the algebraic count of intersections
u\cdot v
between
u
and
v
is given by

u\cdot v=\operatorname{sl}(\gamma)+\operatorname{wind}^{\tau}(e)\leq-1+1=0.

Positivity of intersections then immediately implies that
u
and
v
are disjoint. Note that we could also have proved this assertion using more general intersection theoretic results for punctured pseudoholomorphic curves due to Hutchings
[
Hut02
]
and Siefring
[
Sie11
]
.

Consider now the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
. The facts that all fast planes asymptotic to
\gamma
are embedded and that any two distinct fast planes are disjoint implies that
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
is injective. By Propositions
3.4
and
3.5
it is also proper and a local diffeomorphism. Since we have already established that
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}})>0
, we can deduce that
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
is a diffeomorphism, implying that
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})=1
. It remains to argue that any fast plane in the symplectization is disjoint from the trivial cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\gamma
. Assume by contradiction that
u
is a fast plane intersecting
\mathbb{R}\times\gamma
. Since
u
is asymptotic to
\gamma
, this would imply that
u
intersects the translated plane
u+c
for all
c\gg 0
sufficiently large. However, this is impossible because
u
and
u+c
are distinct and thus disjoint by the above discussion.
∎

Let us now turn to the proof of Theorem
4.3
. If
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})>0
, then there exists an immersed fast plane asymptotic to
\gamma
. Starting from such a plane, it is not hard to construct a positively immersed symplectic disk with boundary
\gamma
. Conversely, if
\gamma
bounds a positively immersed symplectic disk, then
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})>0
by Claim
4.5
. This shows the first assertion of Theorem
4.3
.

Next, we verify the equivalence of statements (
i
) - (
iv
). Suppose that
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})=1
. Then
\gamma
bounds a positively immersed symplectic disk by the first assertion of Theorem
4.3
. By Claim
4.6
, this disk must be embedded. Conversely, it
\gamma
bounds an embedded symplectic disk, then
\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev})=1
by Claim
4.7
. This shows the equivalence of statements (
i
) and (
ii
).

Assume that
\gamma
bounds an embedded symplectic disk. Then Claim
4.7
implies that the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbb{R}\times Y}
is a diffeomorphism onto
\mathbb{R}\times(Y\setminus\gamma)
. In this situation, it is well-known that the projection of each fast plane
u
to
Y
is an embedded disk-like global surface of section with boundary
\gamma
(see
[
HWZ98
,
Hry12
]
). The existence of a disk-like global surface of section with boundary
\gamma
clearly implies that
\gamma
is Hopf. Finally, every Hopf orbit must bound an embedded symplectic disk because every fiber of the Hopf fibration does. This shows the equivalence of statements (
ii
), (
iii
) and (
iv
).
∎

5
Floer theory and Clarke duality

5.1
The Floer complex of the direct action functional

We recall the definition of the Floer complex which is associated to a suitable time-periodic Hamiltonian
H
and time-periodic almost complex structure
J
on
(\mathbb{R}^{2n},\omega_{0})
. Given a Hamiltonian
H\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, let
X_{H}
be the time-periodic Hamiltonian vector field defined by

\imath_{X_{H}}\omega_{0}=-dH,

where
d
denotes differentiation with respect to the spatial variables. We assume that
H
satisfies the following conditions:

(Linear growth of the Hamiltonian vector field)

There exists
c>0
such that
|X_{H}(t,z)|\leq c(1+|z|)
for every
(t,z)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.

(Non-resonance at infinity)

There exist positive numbers
\epsilon>0
and
r>0
such that, for every smooth curve
\gamma:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfying

\|\dot{\gamma}-X_{H}(\cdot,\gamma)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}<\epsilon,

the inequality
\|\gamma\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq r
holds.

(Non-degeneracy)

All 1-periodic orbits
\gamma
of
X_{H}
are nondegenerate, meaning that 1 is not an eigenvalue of the differential of the time-1 flow at
\gamma(0)
.

Remark 5.1
.

Here is a typical class of Hamiltonians which satisfy the first two conditions. Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
be a domain which is uniformly starshaped with respect to the origin. Denote by
H_{X}
the unique positively 2-homogeneous function taking the value 1 on
\partial X
and assume that
H\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n})
satisfies

H(t,z)=\eta H_{X}(z)+\sigma,\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{T},\forall z\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\mbox{ with }|z|\geq R,

where
\eta>0
,
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and
R>0
. Then the Hamltonian vector field of
H
has linear growth. Moreover,
H
is non-resonant at infinity provided that
\eta
is different from all the actions of closed characteristics on
\partial X
. See
[
AK22
, Lemma 4.1]
. Another class of Hamiltonians satisfying these two conditions is described in
[
AK22
, Remark 4.2]
.

By the above assumptions, the set
\mathcal{P}(H)
of 1-periodic orbits of
X_{H}
is finite. Its elements are precisely the critical points of the direct action functional

\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma):=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\gamma^{*}\lambda_{0}-\int_{\mathbb{T}}H(t,\gamma(t))\,dt,\qquad\gamma\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n}),

(5.1)

where
\lambda_{0}
is any primitive of
\omega_{0}
.

Let
J=J(t,z)
be a smooth
\omega_{0}
-compatible time-periodic almost complex structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
which is globally bounded as map into the space of endomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(H)
. The symplectic form
\omega_{0}
and the almost complex structure
J
induce a Hermitian structure on the vector bundle
\gamma^{*}(T\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, which carries the asymptotic operator
A_{H,\gamma}:=-J\nabla_{t}^{X_{H}}
, where
\nabla_{t}^{X_{H}}
is the symplectic connection which is obtained by linearizing the flow of
X_{H}
along
\gamma
. In terms of the natural trivialization of
T\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, we have

A_{H,\gamma}=-J(\cdot,\gamma(\cdot))\frac{d}{dt}-\nabla_{J}^{2}H(\cdot,\gamma(\cdot)),

where
\nabla_{J}^{2}H
denotes the spatial Hessian of
H
with respect to the time-dependent Riemannian structure induced by
J
. The asymptotic operator
A_{H,\gamma}
is nondegenerate and the corresponding path in the linear symplectic group is given by the differential of the flow of
X_{H}
along
\gamma
. We denote by
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma)
the Conley-Zehnder index of this path. The
orientation line of
\gamma

\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma}
is defined to be the orientation line of a Cauchy-Riemann type operator on
\mathbb{C}
which is positively asymptotic to
A_{H,\gamma}
. This is well defined because the space of these Cauchy-Riemann type operators is contractible. The choice of a different
J
produces a canonically isomorphic orientation line
\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma}
.

For every pair of 1-periodic orbits
\gamma_{+}
,
\gamma_{-}
in
\mathcal{P}(H)
, consider the space of Floer cylinders

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-}):=\bigl\{u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})\mid&\;\partial_{s}u+J(t,u)(\partial_{t}u-X_{H}(t,u))=0,\\
&\lim_{s\rightarrow\pm\infty}u(s,\cdot)=\gamma_{\pm}\bigr\}.\end{split}

(5.2)

The energy identity for
u\in\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})
reads

E(u):=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{s}u|_{J_{t}}^{2}\,ds\,dt=\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma_{+})-\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma_{-}),

where
|\cdot|_{J_{t}}^{2}=\omega_{0}(\cdot,J_{t}\cdot)
. Together with the above assumptions of linear growth of
X_{H}
and non-resonance at infinity, the uniform upper bound on the energy implies that the cylinders in
\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})
take values in a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, see
[
AK22
, Proposition 3.1]
. Then a standard argument involving elliptic estimates and the fact that
\omega_{0}
is exact implies that each space
\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})
is pre-compact in the
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}
topology.

The linearization of the Floer equation at a solution
u\in\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})
is a real Cauchy-Riemann type operator
D_{u}
on the Hermitian vector bundle

u^{*}(T\mathbb{R}^{2n})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}

which is asymptotic to the operators
A_{H,\gamma_{\pm}}
for
s\rightarrow\pm\infty
. The operator
D_{u}
is Fredholm of index

\mathop{\mathrm{}}{ind}D_{u}=\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{+})-\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{-}).

If
J
is generic, then all the spaces
\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})
are cut transversely and are hence smooth manifolds of dimension
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{+})-\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{-})
. We fix such a generic
J
.

Now consider the case of index difference
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{+})-\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{-})=1
. The quotient

\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-}):=\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})/\mathbb{R}

by the free
\mathbb{R}
-action
(\sigma,u)\mapsto u(\sigma+\cdot,\cdot)
is discrete and compact, and hence a finite set. The algebraic count of
\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})
naturally takes value in
\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}
. Indeed, let
D_{\gamma_{-}}
be a real Cauchy-Riemann type operator on
\mathbb{C}
which is positively asymptotic to
A_{H,\gamma_{-}}
and consider
u\in\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})
. Then gluing the linearization
D_{u}
with
D_{\gamma_{-}}
produces a Cauchy-Riemann type operator on
\mathbb{C}
which is positively asymptotic to
A_{H,\gamma_{+}}
. Hence the Floer-Hofer kernel gluing operation yields a canonical isomorphism

\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}}\cong\mathfrak{o}_{D_{u}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}.

In other words, the orientation local system of
\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}^{*}
. Since
\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})
is obtained by taking the quotient by the above
\mathbb{R}
-action, its orientation local system is naturally isomorphic to
\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}^{*}
. Here we identify
\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\mathbb{Z}
using the obvious orientation of
\mathbb{R}
. A choice of generator of
\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}^{*}
thus induces on orientation of
\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})
, which in turn gives rise to a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued count. This precisely describes an element of the dual line
(\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}^{*})^{*}\cong\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}
, i.e. yields a canonical count

\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})\in\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}},\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}).

Note that the absolute value
|\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})|\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is well-defined and independent of choices.

The
Floer complex

\partial^{F}:F_{*}(H)\rightarrow F_{*-1}(H)
of
(H,J)
is defined as

F_{k}(H):=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(H)\\
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma)=k\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma},\qquad\partial^{F}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-})\in\mathcal{P}(H)^{2}\\
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{+})-\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{-})=1\end{subarray}}\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{+},\gamma_{-}).

(5.3)

Indeed, a standard argument proves that
\partial^{F}
is a boundary operator. The resulting object is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded chain complex of free Abelian groups
(F_{k}(H),\partial^{F})
which is filtered by the action
\mathcal{A}_{H}
. Changing the generic almost complex structure
J
produces an isomorphic
\mathbb{Z}
-graded and filtered chain complex.

5.2
The Morse complex of the dual action functional

We summarize some facts about Clarke’s dual action functional and its Morse complex. We refer to
[
AK22
, Sections 5 and 6]
for the proofs. Note that we are adopting different sign conventions and are making some other minor changes.

We now assume that the Hamiltonian
H\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is nondegenerate, non-resonant at infinity and satisfies the following condition:

(Quadratic convexity)

There are positive numbers
\underline{h},\overline{h}
such that
\underline{h}I\leq\nabla^{2}H(t,z)\leq\overline{h}I
for every
(t,z)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.

This condition implies the linear growth at infinity of the Hamiltonian vector field. A typical asymptotic behaviour of quadratically convex Hamiltonians is the one described in Remark
5.1
, where now the domain
X
is assumed to be uniformly convex, meaning that its boundary has strictly positive sectional curvature.

Denote by

H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n}):=\Bigl\{\gamma\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})\mid\int_{\mathbb{T}}\gamma(t)\,dt=0\Bigr\}

the Hilbert space of loops in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
of Sobolev regularity
H^{1}
with vanishing mean and by

\mathbb{P}:H^{1}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})\rightarrow H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\qquad\gamma\mapsto\gamma-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\gamma(t)\,dt,

the linear projection onto it along the space of constant loops. The Hilbert space
H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is the domain of Clarke’s dual action functional, which takes the form

\Psi_{H^{*}}(\gamma):=-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\gamma^{*}\lambda_{0}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}H^{*}(t,-J_{0}\gamma^{\prime}(t))\,dt,\qquad\gamma\in H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n}),

where
H^{*}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is the Fenchel conjugate of
H
:

H^{*}(t,z):=\max_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\bigl(w\cdot z-H(t,w)\bigr).

There is a one-to-one correspondence between critical points of the dual action functional and critical points of the direct action functional, i.e. 1-periodic orbits of
X_{H}
: if
\gamma\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is a critical point of
\mathcal{A}_{H}
then
\mathbb{P}(\gamma)
is a critical point of
\Psi_{H^{*}}
and, conversely, if
\gamma\in H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is a critical point of
\Psi_{H^{*}}
then there exists a unique vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that
\gamma+z
is a critical point of
\mathcal{A}_{H}
. The values of the two functionals at critical points coincide:

\Psi_{H^{*}}(\mathbb{P}(\gamma))=\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma)\qquad\forall\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(H).

(5.4)

One advantage of the dual action functional
\Psi_{H^{*}}
is that its critical points have finite Morse index
i_{\mathrm{M}}
: more precisely, we have

i_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbb{P}(\gamma),\Psi_{H^{*}})=\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma)-n,\qquad\forall\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(H).

(5.5)

The functional
\Psi_{H^{*}}
is continuously differentiable on
H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})
but in general only twice Gâteaux differentiable. In order to associate a Morse complex to it, it is useful to restrict it to a suitable finite dimensional submanifold of
H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})
which contains all its critical points and has the property that the restriction of
\Psi_{H^{*}}
to it is smooth. Given a natural number
N
, we consider the orthogonal splitting

\begin{split}H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})=\mathbb{H}_{N}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{N},\qquad\mathbb{H}_{N}&:=\{\gamma\mid\widehat{\gamma}(k)=0\mbox{ if }k<0\mbox{ or }k>N\},\\
\mathbb{H}^{N}&:=\{\gamma\mid\widehat{\gamma}(k)=0\mbox{ if }1\leq k\leq N\},\end{split}

where
\widehat{\gamma}(k)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
are the Fourier coefficients of
\gamma
in the representation

\gamma(t)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{2\pi ktJ_{0}}\widehat{\gamma}(k).

Denote by
\mathbb{P}_{N}
the projector onto the
2nN
-dimensional subspace
\mathbb{H}_{N}
along
\mathbb{H}^{N}
.

If
N
is large enough, then for every
x\in\mathbb{H}_{N}
there exists
Y_{N}(x)\in\mathbb{H}^{N}
such that the restriction of
\Psi_{H^{*}}
to the affine subspace
x+\mathbb{H}^{N}
has a unique critical point
x+Y_{N}(x)
, which is a global minimizer. The map
Y_{N}:\mathbb{H}_{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{N}
is smooth and takes values in the space of smooth loops. We denote by
M\subset H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})
the graph of
Y_{N}
and by
\psi_{H^{*}}
the restriction of
\Psi_{H^{*}}
to
M
:

M:=\{x+Y_{N}(x)\mid x\in\mathbb{H}_{N}\},\qquad\psi_{H^{*}}:=\Psi_{H^{*}}|_{M}.

We have

\psi_{H^{*}}(q)\leq\Psi_{H^{*}}(q+y)\qquad\forall q\in M,\;\forall y\in\mathbb{H}^{N}.

(5.6)

The function
\psi_{H^{*}}
on the finite dimensional manifold
M
is smooth, Morse and satisfies the Palais-Smale condition, once
M
is endowed with the Riemannian structure induced by the ambient Hilbert space
H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})
. The critical points of
\psi_{H^{*}}
coincide with the critical points of
\Psi_{H^{*}}
and have the same Morse index. By the above considerations, we then have

i_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbb{P}(\gamma)):=i_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathbb{P}(\gamma),\psi_{H^{*}})=\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma)-n,\qquad\forall\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(H).

(5.7)

By the non-degeneracy and non-resonance assumption,
\psi_{H^{*}}
has finitely many critical points. Given a Riemannian metric
g
on
M
which is uniformly equivalent to the one induced by the ambient Hilbert space, we denote by
\phi^{t}_{-\nabla\psi_{H^{*}}}
the negative gradient flow of
\psi_{H^{*}}
with respect to
g
and by

\begin{split}W^{u}(p)&:=\{q\in M\mid\phi^{t}_{-\nabla\psi_{H^{*}}}(q)\rightarrow p\mbox{ for }t\rightarrow-\infty\},\\
W^{s}(p)&:=\{q\in M\mid\phi^{t}_{-\nabla\psi_{H^{*}}}(q)\rightarrow p\mbox{ for }t\rightarrow+\infty\},\end{split}

the unstable and stable manifold of the critical point
p
of
\psi_{H^{*}}
with respect to this flow. The unstable manifold of
p
has dimension
i_{\mathrm{M}}(p)
.

When the metric
g
is generic, unstable and stable manifolds of each pair of critical points intersect transversally and
\psi_{H^{*}}
has a well-defined Morse complex
\{M_{*}(\psi_{H^{*}}),\partial^{M}\}
over
\mathbb{Z}
, whose definition we briefly recall.

The orientation line of a critical point
p
of
\psi_{H^{*}}
is defined to be

\mathfrak{o}_{p}:=\mathfrak{o}_{T_{p}W^{u}(p)},

and we consider the free Abelian group

M_{k}(\psi_{H^{*}}):=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathrm{crit}(\psi_{H^{*}})\\
i_{\mathrm{M}}(p)=k\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{o}_{p}.

Since the unstable manifold of
p
is orientable and connected, we obtain canonical isomorphisms

\mathfrak{o}_{T_{x}W^{u}(p)}\cong\mathfrak{o}_{p}\qquad\forall x\in W^{u}(p).

(5.8)

Since
T_{p}M=T_{p}W^{u}(p)\oplus T_{p}W^{s}(p)
, we also have canonical isomorphisms

\mathfrak{o}_{\frac{T_{x}M}{T_{x}W^{s}(p)}}\cong\mathfrak{o}_{\frac{T_{p}M}{T_{p}W^{s}(p)}}\cong\mathfrak{o}_{T_{p}W^{u}(p)}=\mathfrak{o}_{p}\qquad\forall x\in W^{s}(p).

(5.9)

Let
x\in W^{u}(p_{+})\cap W^{s}(p_{-})
. From the Morse-Smale transversality condition
T_{x}W^{u}(p_{+})+T_{x}W^{s}(p_{-})=T_{x}M
, we obtain the exact sequence

0\rightarrow T_{x}\bigl(W^{u}(p_{+})\cap W^{s}(p_{-})\bigr)\rightarrow T_{x}W^{u}(p_{+})\rightarrow\frac{T_{x}M}{T_{x}W^{s}(p_{-})}\rightarrow 0,

where the first map is the inclusion and the second one the restriction of the quotient projection. Together with (
5.8
) and (
5.9
), the above exact sequence induces the canonical isomorphism

\mathfrak{o}_{p_{+}}\cong\mathfrak{o}_{T_{x}(W^{u}(p_{+})\cap W^{s}(p_{-}))}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{p_{-}}.

(5.10)

This means that the orientation local system of
W^{u}(p_{+})\cap W^{s}(p_{-})
is canonically isomorphic to
\mathfrak{o}_{p_{+}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{p_{-}}^{*}
. The gradient flow of
\nabla\psi_{H^{*}}
induces an
\mathbb{R}
-action on
W^{u}(p_{+})\cap W^{s}(p_{-})
, which is free whenever
p_{+}\neq p_{-}
. In this case, the orientation local system of the quotient

\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{M}}(p_{+},p_{-})\coloneqq W^{u}(p_{+})\cap W^{s}(p_{-})/\mathbb{R}

is canonically isomorphic to
\mathfrak{o}_{p_{+}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{p_{-}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}\cong\mathfrak{o}_{p_{+}}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{p_{-}}^{*}
, where we again identify
\mathfrak{o}_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\mathbb{Z}
using the standard orientation of
\mathbb{R}
.

If
i_{\mathrm{M}}(p_{+})-i_{\mathrm{M}}(p_{-})=1
, then
\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{M}}(p_{+},p_{-})
is
0
-dimensional and we have a canonical count (cf. Section
5.1
)

\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{M}}(p_{+},p_{-})\in\mathfrak{o}_{p_{+}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{p_{-}}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathfrak{o}_{p_{+}},\mathfrak{o}_{p_{-}}).

As in Section
5.1
, the absolute value
|\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{M}}(p_{+},p_{-})|\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
does not depend on choices. The homomorphism
\partial^{M}:M_{*}(\psi_{H^{*}})\rightarrow M_{*-1}(\psi_{H^{*}})
is defined by the formula

\partial^{M}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(p_{+},p_{-})\in\mathrm{crit}(\psi_{H^{*}})^{2}\\
i_{\mathrm{M}}(p_{+})-i_{\mathrm{M}}(p_{-})=1\end{subarray}}\;\;\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{M}}(p_{+},p_{-}).

A standard argument proves that
\partial^{M}
is a boundary operator and that the
k
-th homology group of the Morse complex
\{M_{*}(\psi_{H^{*}}),\partial^{M}\}
is isomorphic to the singular homology group
H_{k}(\mathbb{H}_{N},\{\psi_{H^{*}}<c\})
, where
c
is a real number which is smaller than the smallest critical value of
\psi_{H^{*}}
. It is clear from the construction that we get an isomorphic filtered chain complex with a different choice of large
N\in\mathbb{N}
, which corresponds to the isomorphism induced by
(\mathbb{H}_{N},\{\psi_{H^{*}}^{N}<c\})\subset(\mathbb{H}_{N^{\prime}},\{\psi_{H^{*}}^{N^{\prime}}<c\})
for
N<N^{\prime}
on the singular homology side, where we added the superscript
N
to
\psi_{H^{*}}^{N}
for distinction.

Remark 5.2
.

Note that we are defining the Morse complex using the negative gradient flow of
\psi_{H^{*}}
, but we are orienting the flow lines by the positive gradient. The reason is that this choice simplifies the form of the isomorphism with Floer homology, the reason being that the Floer equation appearing in (
5.2
) is a positive gradient equation for the action functional
\mathcal{A}_{H}
.

5.3
An isomorphism

Assume that the Hamiltonian
H\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is nondegenerate, non-resonant at infinity and quadratically convex. Fixing a generic time-periodic almost complex structure
J
on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and a generic Riemann metric
g
on
M
as above, both the Floer complex of
(H,J)
and the Morse complex of
(\psi_{H^{*}},g)
are well defined. We then have the following result.

Theorem 5.3
.

There is a chain complex isomorphism

\Theta_{*}:M_{*}(\psi_{H^{*}})\rightarrow F_{*+n}(H)

preserving the action filtrations.

The above theorem is proven in
[
AK22
]
in the case of
\mathbb{Z}/2
coefficients. Its upgrade to integer coefficients is proven in Appendix
B
below, after some preliminaries about orientations of certain Fredholm operators which are discussed in Appendix
A
.

Remark 5.4
.

A consequence of the above theorem is that if
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
is a convex body then the capacity from symplectic homology with integer coefficients of
X
coincides with the minimal action of closed generalized characteristics on
\partial X
. This is proven in
[
AK22
, Section 11]
for the symplectic homology with
\mathbb{Z}/2
coefficients. The same proof, now building on Theorem
5.3
, gives us the desired result.

6
Floer cylinders descending from the systole

Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
be a uniformly convex domain. Throughout this section, we assume that
\partial X
has a unique closed characteristic
\gamma
of minimal action and that
\gamma
is nondegenerate.

Up to a translation, we may assume that the interior of
X
contains the origin and we denote by
H_{X}:\mathbb{R}^{2n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the positively 2-homogeneous function taking the value
1
on
\partial X
. Since the unique closed characteristic of minimal action is nondegenerate, we can find a number
\eta>\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
such that no closed characteristics of
\partial X
have action in the interval
(\mathcal{A}(\gamma),\eta]
. We consider a smooth function
k:[0,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

k|_{[0,\frac{1}{4}]}=0,\quad k^{\prime\prime}\geq 0,\quad k^{\prime}(1)=\mathcal{A}(\gamma),\quad k^{\prime\prime}(1)>0,\quad k^{\prime}|_{[2,+\infty)}=\eta.

The smooth function
k\circ H_{X}
is quadratically convex away from the rescaled domain
\frac{1}{2}X
, on which it vanishes identically. We can modify
k\circ H_{X}
in a small neighborhood of
\frac{1}{2}X
and obtain a smooth function
\tilde{K}
which is quadratically convex on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and achieves its minimum at the origin, with
\tilde{K}(0)<0
and Hessian at
0
with small positive eigenvalues. The fixed point set of the time-
1
map of the Hamiltonian flow of
\tilde{K}
consists of the point
z_{0}=0
, which defines a nondegenerate constant orbit with Conley-Zehnder index
\mathrm{CZ}(z_{0})=n
, and the circle
\gamma
, which is a degenerate orbit with nullity 1. Let
h\in\mathbb{Z}
be the Conley-Zehnder index of
\gamma
, which we recall is extended to degenerate orbits by lower semicontinuity. It follows from Ekeland’s work
[
Eke90
]
that
h=n+1
, and we will recover this fact below.

By
[
FCHW96
, Proposition 2.2]
, we can perturb
\tilde{K}
near
\gamma
in a time-dependent way and obtain a smooth function
K:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that:

(i)

K(t,z)=\eta H_{X}(z)+\sigma
outside of a compact subset of
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, where
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
;

(ii)

K
is nondegenerate, non-resonant at infinity (see Remark
5.1
), and quadratically convex;

(iii)

X_{K}
has three 1-periodic orbits: the constant orbit
z_{0}=0
and two non-constant orbits
\gamma_{-}
and
\gamma_{+}
near
\gamma
;

(iv)

\mathrm{CZ}(z_{0})=n
,
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{-})=h
, and
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{+})=h+1
.

By (ii), the Clarke dual functional
\Psi_{K^{*}}
and its finite dimensional reduction
\psi_{K^{*}}
are well-defined, and the latter function has a well-defined Morse complex.
By (iii) and (iv), the function
\psi_{K^{*}}
has three critical points, which are given by the projections by
\mathbb{P}
of
z_{0}
,
\gamma_{-}
and
\gamma_{+}
and have Morse index 0,
h-n
and
h+1-n
, respectively, see (
5.7
).

Assumption (i) implies that
\psi_{K^{*}}
is unbounded from below. For convenience of the reader, we repeat the argument in
[
AK22
, Remark 11.3]
here. A suitable reparametrization
\widehat{\gamma}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2n}
of the closed characteristic
\gamma
satisfies

\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}(t)=\mathcal{A}(\gamma)X_{H_{X}}(\widehat{\gamma}(t)),

and hence is a 1-periodic orbit of the Hamiltonian flow defined by

\widehat{H}:=\mathcal{A}(\gamma)H_{X}.

The fact that
\widehat{H}
is 2-homogeneous implies that the critical point
\widehat{\gamma}
of
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{H}}
has vanishing Hamiltonian action and hence

\Psi_{\widehat{H}^{*}}(\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\gamma}))=\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{H}}(\widehat{\gamma})=0.

(6.1)

By (i) we have

K(t,z)\geq\eta H_{X}(z)-c=\theta\widehat{H}(z)-c\qquad\forall(t,z)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n},

where
\theta:=\frac{\eta}{\mathcal{A}(\gamma)}>1
and
c
is suitably large. Then

K^{*}(t,z)\leq\theta\widehat{H}^{*}\left(\frac{z}{\theta}\right)+c=\frac{1}{\theta}\widehat{H}^{*}(z)+c\qquad\forall(t,z)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n},

and hence for every
s>0
we have

\begin{split}\Psi_{K^{*}}(s\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\gamma}))&=-\mathcal{A}(s\widehat{\gamma})+\int_{\mathbb{T}}K^{*}(t,-sJ_{0}\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}(t))\,dt\leq-s^{2}\mathcal{A}(\gamma)+\frac{1}{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widehat{H}^{*}(-sJ_{0}\widehat{\gamma}^{\prime}(t))\,dt+c\\
&=\frac{s^{2}}{\theta}\Psi_{\widehat{H}^{*}}(\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\gamma}))+\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)\mathcal{A}(\gamma)s^{2}+c=\left(\frac{1}{\theta}-1\right)\mathcal{A}(\gamma)s^{2}+c,\end{split}

where in the last equality we have used (
6.1
). Since
\theta>1
, the above inequality implies that
\Psi_{K^{*}}(s\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\gamma}))
tends to
-\infty
for
s\rightarrow+\infty
and hence
\Psi_{K^{*}}
is unbounded from below. By (
5.6
), the reduced function
\psi_{K^{*}}
is unbounded from below as well, as claimed.

This implies that the relative homology group
H_{0}(M,\{\psi_{K^{*}}<a\})
vanishes for every
a\in\mathbb{R}
. If
a
is smaller than the minimum of
\psi_{K^{*}}
over its critical set, then the Morse homology of
\psi_{K^{*}}
is isomorphic to
H_{0}(M,\{\psi_{K^{*}}<a\})
. The fact that the latter group vanishes implies that the degree zero cycle defined by
\mathbb{P}(z_{0})
is a boundary. Therefore, the function
\psi_{K^{*}}
must have a critical point of Morse index one and this is necessarily
\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{-})
, because if
\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{+})
had index one, then
\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{-})
would be a second critical point of index zero and the Morse homology of
\psi_{K^{*}}
in degree zero would not vanish. We deduce that
h=n+1
, as claimed above. The fact that the degree zero cycle defined by
\mathbb{P}(z_{0})
is a boundary implies that
|\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{M}}(\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{-}),\mathbb{P}(z_{0}))|=1
. We also observe that, since the unstable manifold of
\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{-})
is one-dimensional, this implies that one of the two branches of this unstable manifold converges to
\mathbb{P}(z_{0})
while on the other branch
\psi_{K^{*}}
tends to
-\infty
.

Let
J
be a globally bounded time-periodic
\omega_{0}
-compatible almost complex structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that
(K,J)
is regular, meaning that all the moduli spaces of Floer cylinders are cut transversally. Since
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{-})=n+1
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{-},z_{0})
is
0
-dimensional and compact. From Theorem
5.3
and the fact that
|\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{M}}(\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{-}),\mathbb{P}(z_{0}))|=1
, we conclude that
|\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{-},z_{0})|=1
. We summarize our findings into the following theorem.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
K:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth Hamiltonian satisfying the above conditions (i)-(iv) and assume that the pair
(K,J)
is regular. Then
h=n+1
and
|\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{-},z_{0})|=1
.

Remark 6.2
.

The above proof also shows that in the Morse complex of
\psi_{K^{*}}
the boundary of the chain which is associated to the critical point
\mathbb{P}(\gamma_{+})
is zero. This fact can also be deduced from the analogous statement for the chain defined by
\gamma_{+}
in the Floer complex of
(K,J)
, which is proven in the above mentioned
[
FCHW96
, Proposition 2.2]
. This leads to a less direct but shorter proof of the fact that the Clarke dual functional is unbounded from below when condition (i) holds. See
[
AK22
, Lemma 11.2]
.

Remark 6.3
.

Let us also sketch a third way of determining the Morse complex of
\psi_{K^{*}}
. Consider the finite dimensional reduction
\psi_{\tilde{K}^{*}}
of the Clarke dual action functional of the autonomous Hamiltonian
\tilde{K}
. Recall that
K
was obtained from
\tilde{K}
by a small time dependent perturbation. The function
\psi_{\tilde{K}^{*}}
is a Morse-Bott function on a manifold diffeomorphic to some finite dimensional vector space
V
and satisfies the Palais-Smale condition. It has a local minimum corresponding to the constant orbit
z_{0}
and a
\mathbb{T}
-family of critical points of some index
k\geq 1
corresponding to
\gamma
. Hence
V
is obtained from the sublevel set
\left\{\psi_{\tilde{K}^{*}}<a\right\}
for a sufficiently low value of
a
by attaching a single
0
-handle and a
\mathbb{T}
-family of
k
-handles. If the sublevel set
\left\{\psi_{\tilde{K}^{*}}<a\right\}
were empty, then
V
would be homotopy equivalent to the one-point compactification of
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
, contradicting the fact that
V
is contractible. Hence
\left\{\psi_{\tilde{K}^{*}}<a\right\}
is non-empty. But then the only way to turn the union of
\left\{\psi_{\tilde{K}^{*}}<a\right\}
and the
0
-handle corresponding to
z_{0}
into a connected space is to attach a
\mathbb{T}
-family of
1
-handles connecting the two sets. The unstable manifold of the
\mathbb{T}
-family of index
1
critical points is an infinite cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
. On one side, this cylinder converges to the local minimum corresponding to
z_{0}
, and on the other side the value of
\psi_{\tilde{K}^{*}}
tends to
-\infty
. The function
\psi_{K^{*}}
is obtained from
\psi_{\tilde{K}^{*}}
by a small perturbation splitting the
\mathbb{T}
-family of index
1
critical points into two critical points of indices
1
and
2
, respectively. Clearly, there is a unique gradient flow line connecting the index
1
critical point and the local minimum.

7
Pseudoholomorphic planes descending from the systole

Throughout this section, let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
be a uniformly convex domain containing the origin in its interior. As in Section
6
, we assume that the Reeb flow of
\lambda
on
Y=\partial X
has a unique periodic orbit
\gamma
of minimal action and that
\gamma
is nondegenerate. Recall that in this situation we must have
\operatorname{CZ}(\gamma)=n+1
.

Let
J
be an admissible almost complex structure on the symplectic completion
\hat{X}
and let
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,J)
denote the moduli space of unparametrized
J
-holomorphic planes with one marked point positively asymptotic to
\gamma
. The virtual dimension of this moduli space is
2n
. Since
\gamma
is a simple orbit, planes asymptotic to
\gamma
are automatically somewhere injective. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,J)
is a smooth
2n
-manifold for generic
J
. The marked point gives rise to an evaluation map

\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}:\mathcal{M}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,J)\rightarrow\hat{X}.

(7.1)

This evaluation map is proper. Indeed, since
\gamma
has minimal action, the SFT limit of a sequence of planes asypmtotic to
\gamma
can only have one single layer. It must be a plane asymptotic to
\gamma
either in the symplectization of
Y
or in the completion
\hat{X}
. Using this observation, properness of
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
follows from an argument analogous to the one given in the proof of Proposition
3.4
.

As a consequence of the above discussion, we see that the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
has a well-defined degree for generic
J
. The sign of this degree depends on a choice of orientation of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,J)
. Note that the orientation local system of
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,J)
is canonically isomorphic to the orientation line
\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma}
of the Reeb orbit
\gamma
(see Section
2.5
). In order to avoid a choice of orientation, we can regard the degree of
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
as an element of
\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma}^{*}
, i.e.

\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}})\in\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma}^{*}.

Note that the absolute value
|\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}})|\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
does not depend on a choice of orientation. The degree of
\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}}
is independent of the choice of generic
J
. This is a consequence of the fact that for a family of almost complex structures
(J_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
, the evaluation map

\operatorname{ev}_{[0,1]\times\hat{X}}:\mathcal{M}_{1}(\hat{X},\gamma,(J_{t})_{t\in[0,1]})\rightarrow[0,1]\times\hat{X}\quad(t,[u,z])\mapsto(t,u(z))

is proper (cf. Propostion
3.8
). Again, properness of
\operatorname{ev}_{[0,1]\times\hat{X}}
relies on
\gamma
being the Reeb orbit of minimal action.

Let us point out that, in the case
n=2
, we have
\operatorname{CZ}(\gamma)=3
, which implies that all pseudoholomorphic planes positively asymptotic to
\gamma
are automatically fast. This means that in this case, the evaluation map (
7.1
) is the same as the one discussed in Section
3
.

The goal of the present section is to prove the following result.

Theorem 7.1
.

We have
|\operatorname{deg}(\operatorname{ev}_{\hat{X}})|=1
.

The proof of Theorem
7.1
will occupy the rest of this section. We sketch an outline below.

Step 1.
The key idea of the proof is to transform Floer cylinders of the Hamiltonian
K
we studied in Theorem
6.1
into pseudoholomorphic planes using the neck stretching technique. However,
K
is not suitable for neck stretching, so we consider another Hamiltonian
H
. The Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}
of
H
has
\gamma_{-}
and
z_{0}
as nondegenerate 1-periodic orbits. Here,
z_{0}
is the constant orbit mapping to the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, and
\gamma_{-}
is the orbit with
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{-})=n+1
that splits off from
\gamma
as in Section
6
. The Hamiltonian
H
is zero on
X\setminus U_{z_{0}}
, where
U_{z_{0}}
is a neighborhood of
z_{0}
, so that Floer cylinders of
H
are pseudoholomorphic on this region. In Proposition
7.2
, we prove that the absolute value of the algebraic count of Floer cylinders of
H
descending from
\gamma_{-}
to
z_{0}
is equal to
1
. This follows from Theorem
6.1
and a continuation argument. This is the content of Section
7.1
.

Step 2.
We take an irrational ellipsoid
E
such that
\overline{U}_{z_{0}}\subset\mathrm{int}(E)
and
E\subset\mathrm{int}(X)
. We show that if one stretches the neck along the hypersurfaces
F:=\partial E
and
Y=\partial X
, any Floer cylinder of
H
descending from
\gamma_{-}
to
z_{0}
as in Step 1 breaks into three pieces. The middle piece is a pseudoholomorphic cylinder in the symplectic completion
\hat{M}
of the Liouville cobordism
M:=X\setminus\mathrm{int}(E)
between
Y
and
F
. This cylinder is positively asymptotic to
\gamma
and negatively asymptotic to the minimal action orbit
\gamma_{E}
on
F
. A gluing theorem implies that the absolute value of the algebraic count of such pseudoholomorphic cylinders is equal to
1
. We introduce and investigate the relevant moduli spaces in Sections
7.2
,
7.3
, and
7.4
. The gluing result is discussed in Section
7.5
.

Step 3.
Let
\hat{E}
be the symplectic completion of
E
. For any point
z\in\hat{E}
, the absolute value of the algebraic count of pseudoholomorphic planes in
\hat{E}
passing through
z
and asymptotic to
\gamma_{E}
is equal to
1
. By gluing such a plane and a cylinder in
\hat{M}
as in Step 2, we obtain a pseudoholomorphic plane in the symplectic completion
\hat{X}
passing through
z
and asymptotic to
\gamma
. A gluing theorem implies that the absolute value of the algebraic count of such planes is equal to
1
, which proves Theorem
7.1
. This is carried out in Section
7.6
.

7.1
Floer cylinders

Let
\eta>\mathcal{A}(\gamma)=\mathcal{A}_{\min}(X)
be a constant such that
\gamma
is the unique periodic orbit of
\lambda
with action smaller than or equal to
\eta
. We fix a constant
a\in(1,2)
sufficiently close to
1
and consider a smooth function
h:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\begin{split}&h=0\mbox{ on a neighborhood of }[0,1],\\
h^{\prime\prime}\geq 0,&\quad h^{\prime}(a)=\mathcal{A}(\gamma),\quad h^{\prime\prime}(a)>0,\quad h^{\prime}|_{[2,+\infty)}=\eta.\end{split}

(7.2)

Then the non-constant
1
-periodic orbits of the Hamiltonian flow of
h\circ H_{X}
are precisely given by
\sqrt{a}\gamma(\mathcal{A}(\gamma)(t+t_{0}))
for
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
, where as before
H_{X}
is the positively 2-homogeneous function on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
taking the value 1 on
\partial X
. We modify
h\circ H_{X}
in a small neighborhood
U_{z_{0}}
of the origin
z_{0}
by adding a non-positive
C^{2}
-small perturbation such that the resulting Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
has the property that the origin
z_{0}
is the unique minimum point of
\tilde{H}
, satisfies
\tilde{H}(z_{0})<0
, and is nondegenerate as a 1-periodic orbit of
X_{\tilde{H}}
. Moreover, we can arrange the perturbation so that no new non-constant
1
-periodic orbits are created and
z_{0}
is the only constant
1
-periodic orbit of
\tilde{H}
outside of
\tilde{H}^{-1}(0)
.

We further perturb
\tilde{H}
to
H\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n})
in a small neighborhood of
\sqrt{a}\gamma
as in Section
6
so that
X_{H}
has precisely two non-constant 1-periodic orbits
\gamma_{-}
and
\gamma_{+}
, both of which are nondegenerate and moreover satisfy
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{-})=n+1
,
\mathrm{CZ}(\gamma_{+})=n+2
, and
\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma_{-})<\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma_{+})
. Note that the 1-periodic orbits of
X_{H}
are
z_{0}
,
\gamma_{-}
,
\gamma_{+}
, and the constant orbits in the region
H^{-1}(0)
. The first three are nondegenerate and the action
\mathcal{A}_{H}
takes positive values on them, while the constant orbits in
H^{-1}(0)
are degenerate and have vanishing action. Note that, by the convexity of
h
,

\mathcal{A}_{h\circ H_{X}}(\sqrt{a}\gamma)=a\mathcal{A}(\gamma)-h(a)>a\mathcal{A}(\gamma)-h^{\prime}(a)(a-1)=\mathcal{A}(\gamma),

and that, by taking
a\in(1,2)
close to
1
, we can make
\mathcal{A}_{h\circ H_{X}}(\sqrt{a}\gamma)
arbitrarily close to
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
, and in turn
\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma_{-})
and
\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma_{+})
also arbitrarily close to
\mathcal{A}(\gamma)
. In particular, we can arrange that
\gamma
is the only periodic Reeb orbit of
\lambda
with action smaller than
\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma_{-})
.

Consider a compatible time-periodic almost complex structure
J
on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
as in Section
5.1
. As defined in (
5.2
), we let
{\mathcal{M}}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{-},z_{0};H,J)
denote the moduli space of Floer cylinders of
H
which are positively asymptotic to
\gamma_{-}
and negatively asymptotic to
z_{0}
. Moreover, we let

{\mathcal{M}}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{-},z_{0};H,J):={\mathcal{M}}_{\partial^{F}}^{\#}(\gamma_{-},z_{0};H,J)/\mathbb{R},

denote the quotient space, where the
\mathbb{R}
-action is given by
(\sigma,u)\mapsto u(\sigma+\cdot,\cdot)
. The virtual dimension of this quotient space is given by
\operatorname{CZ}(\gamma_{-})-\operatorname{CZ}(z_{0})-1=0
. As in Section
5.1
, we have a well-defined algebraic count
\#\mathcal{M}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{-},z_{0};H,J)\in\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}}^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{o}_{z_{0}}
for generic
J
. The goal of this subsection is to show the following:

Proposition 7.2
.

For generic
J
, we have
|\#{\mathcal{M}}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{-},z_{0};H,J)|=1
.

Proof.

Fix a generic
J
. Since
H
has only finitely many
1
-periodic orbits of positive action, all of which are nondegenerate, we have a well-defined Floer complex
F_{*}^{(0,\infty)}(H)
in the positive action window
(0,\infty)
. This complex is supported in degrees
n
,
n+1
and
n+2
and we have

F_{n}^{(0,\infty)}(H)=\mathfrak{o}_{z_{0}},\quad F_{n+1}^{(0,\infty)}(H)=\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{-}},\quad F_{n+2}^{(0,\infty)}(H)=\mathfrak{o}_{\gamma_{+}}.

The statement that
|\#{\mathcal{M}}_{\partial^{F}}(\gamma_{-},z_{0};H,J)|=1
is equivalent to saying that
F_{n}^{(0,\infty)}(H)
is contained in the image of the boundary operator.

Let us choose a Hamiltonian
K\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n})
for which properties (
i
)-(
iv
) in Section
6
hold and which in addition satisfies:

•

K\leq H
,

•

K(z_{0})=H(z_{0})
.

The condition
K\leq H
allows for the construction of a continuation chain homomorphism

\mathfrak{c}:F_{*}^{(0,\infty)}(K)\rightarrow F_{*}^{(0,\infty)}(H)

perserving degree and action filtration, as we recall below. It follows from Theorem
6.1
that
F_{n}^{(0,\infty)}(K)=\mathfrak{o}_{z_{0}}
is contained in the image of the Floer differential of the complex
F_{*}^{(0,\infty)}(K)
. Our goal is to show that
\mathfrak{c}
restricts to an isomorphism

\mathfrak{c}_{n}:F_{n}^{(0,\infty)}(K)\rightarrow F_{n}^{(0,\infty)}(H).

Since
\mathfrak{c}
is a chain homomorphism, this would imply that
F_{n}^{(0,\infty)}(H)
is contained in the image of the Floer differential of the complex
F_{*}^{(0,\infty)}(H)
as desired.

The chain homomorphism
\mathfrak{c}
is constructed as follows. We interpolate between
K
and
H
via a homotopy
G_{s}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n})
smoothly parametrized by
s\in\mathbb{R}
such that

•

\partial_{s}G_{s}\leq 0
,

•

G_{s}=H
for
s\leq 0
and
G_{s}=K
for
s\geq 0
,

•

X_{G_{s}}
has linear growth with constants uniform in
s
.

Moreover, we choose a uniformly bounded homotopy of almost complex structures
(J_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{R}}
starting and ending at
J
. For every pair of 1-periodic orbits
(x_{+},x_{-})\in\mathcal{P}(K)\times\mathcal{P}(H)
of positive action, consider the space of continuation Floer cylinders

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{c}}(x_{+},x_{-}):=\bigl\{u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2n})\mid&\;\partial_{s}u+J_{s}(t,u)(\partial_{t}u-X_{G_{s}}(t,u))=0,\\
